## Reasoning

**Analysis of the mission:**
- Before switch: penalized for moving → robots should be STILL
- After switch: penalized for moving → robots should STILL... wait, no.
- Before switch: score += 1 per STATIONARY robot → want robots MOVING before switch
- After switch: score += 1 per MOVING robot → want robots STATIONARY after switch

So the optimal strategy is:
1. Before switch time (steps 0-~500): robots should MOVE
2. After switch time: robots should STOP

The green wall lights up at switch time. So the trigger for stopping is seeing GREEN (color 2 in ARGoS, but let me check - the problem says color IDs 1-6, green is likely 2).

Wait, looking at the champion: it's just pure exploration (B=0, always moving). Score 11463. But pure stopping (B=1) would be worse because before the switch, stationary = penalty.

Actually wait - let me re-read. "Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary." So before switch, stationary is BAD. After switch, moving is BAD.

So ideal: move before switch, stop after switch. The green wall appearing is the signal for the switch.

The champion (always moving, score ~11464) does well because:
- Before switch (~500 steps): no penalty (moving = good)
- After switch (~700 steps): all 20 robots moving = 20 per step × 700 steps = 14000 penalty

A robot that stops after seeing green would score:
- Before switch: 0 penalty (moving)
- After switch: 0 penalty (stopped)
= near 0!

The key insight: use GoToColor green (clr=2) to approach green wall, but actually we want to STOP when we see green. So: State 0 = Exploration (moving), transition to State 1 = Stop when perceiving green (C=7, l=2 for green).

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The champion is a 2-state FSM: Explore(rwm=50) → Stop, triggered by seeing green (C=7, l=2, p=0.95) OR a tiny random probability (p=0.001). The stop state has a very rare random back-transition (p=0.001).

The pattern is clear:
1. Move (explore) before the green wall appears
2. Stop when green is detected (the switch signal)
3. Stay stopped (very low probability of returning to explore)

**What works:**
- rwm=50 (moderate turning) beats rwm=92 (too much turning), rwm=20, rwm=30
- p=0.95 for green detection beats p=0.9 or p=1.0
- Having a secondary random transition (p=0.001) from explore to stop helps slightly
- Very low p in stop→explore back-transition (p=0.001) is best

**Exploitation (fine-tuning the champion):**
- Try p=0.99, p=1.0 for green detection
- Try different rwm values near 50 (40, 45, 55, 60)
- Try p=0.0005 or p=0.0001 for back-transition (even rarer)
- Try adding a neighbour-count condition as second explore-exit trigger
- Try p=0.002 vs p=0.001 for the random explore→stop trigger

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: Explore → Repulsion (spread out, better chance of seeing green) → Stop
- 2-state with attraction in explore phase (cluster together)
- Try GoAwayColor(green) as explore behavior (would move away from walls, keeping robots in center for spread)

**LED color note:** Valid values are {0,4,5,6} — avoid 1,2,3.

Actually, adding a neighbour condition to the explore→stop transition doesn't make much sense here. Let me think of better exploration alternatives.

Better exploration ideas:
- 3-state: Explore → Stop(1) when see green; Stop → Explore when random tiny; Explore2 with different rwm
- The issue is that not all robots will see the green wall immediately. Robots near the green wall see it. Others may not. So robots that don't see green will keep exploring (good, no penalty). Robots that see green stop (good, no penalty). 

One concern: robots might stop too early due to the random p=0.001 trigger. At 10 steps/sec, over 500 steps, prob of NOT firing = (1-0.001)^500 ≈ 0.607. So ~40% of robots stop randomly before the switch! That's bad for phase 1 score.

Actually wait - p=0.001 per step, 500 steps: expected stops = 0.001 × 500 = 0.5 robots stop early. With 20 robots, about 10 on average stop early (since each has 40% chance of stopping). That seems high but the champion still scores well...

Maybe p=0.0001 would be better. Let me try very small probabilities.

Also: what if the random back-transition from stop→explore has p=0.001, and some robots stop early and then restart? That's also adding complexity.

Let me try even smaller random probabilities: p=0.0001.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

The champion is: rwm=50, p=0.95 green detection, p=0.002 random explore→stop, p=0.001 stop→explore back-transition.

Interesting findings:
- p=0.002 random trigger beats p=0.001 (champion vs rank 8 which has same but p=0.001 random)
- p=0.0005 back-transition (rank 2) is close to champion
- rwm=40 (rank 3) is better than rwm=45 (rank 4), so lower turning might help
- p=0.0001 random is too low (ranks 11, 13) - robots don't stop early enough on green detection alone
- p=1.0 green detection is slightly worse than p=0.95 (ranks 6-7 vs champion)
- The unreachable-state-3 machine (rank 9) ties with rank 8 - the 3rd state doesn't matter if unreachable

**Key insights:**
1. The random explore→stop trigger at p=0.002 is slightly better than p=0.001 - it ensures more robots stop even without seeing green
2. The back-transition p=0.001 seems OK; p=0.0005 is competitive
3. rwm around 40-50 is the sweet spot
4. p=0.95 for green detection is better than higher values

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Vary rwm around 40-50 (try 35, 42, 47, 50)
- Vary random trigger p around 0.002-0.003 (try 0.0015, 0.0025, 0.003)
- Vary back-transition p (try 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002)
- Try p=0.95 vs p=0.90 for green detection

**Exploration:**
- Try having the stop state also respond to green (to "reinforce" staying stopped)
- Try 3-state with two different explore modes both detecting green
- Try adding a neighbour condition to encourage clustering before stopping

For 2-state machines, destination indexing is trivial: from state 0, D=0→state1; from state 1, D=0→state0.

For 3-state machines:
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 4:**

The champion: rwm=40, p=0.95 green, p=0.002 random explore→stop, p=0.0005 stop→explore back.

Clear patterns:
1. **rwm=40** is the sweet spot (beats 35, 42, 45, 47, 50)
2. **p=0.0005 back-transition** beats p=0.001 (champion vs rank 4)
3. **p=0.002 random trigger** is consistently good
4. **p=0.95 green detection** remains best
5. High back-transition p=0.002 is bad (rank 13) - robots restart too often after stopping

**Exploitation focus:**
- Fine-tune rwm around 40: try 38, 36, 43
- Try back-transition p even lower: 0.0002, 0.0003
- Try random trigger p: 0.0025, 0.0018
- Try rwm=35 with p=0.0005 back (rank 3 had p=0.001, might be better with 0.0005)
- Try p=0.90 green detection with rwm=40

**Exploration:**
- 3-state dual-explore with rwm=40 as primary (the 3-state rank 12 was decent)
- Try adding a "see green while stopped → reinforce stop" by having stop→explore only on neighbour condition (very unlikely)

**Important note on back-transition:** p=0.0005 per step. Over 700 steps after switch: prob of NOT restarting = (1-0.0005)^700 ≈ 0.70. So ~30% of robots restart once - that's bad but manageable. p=0.0002 would give ~87% staying stopped.

For 3-state machines, destination indexing:
- From state 0 (3 states): others=[1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: others=[0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2  
- From state 2: others=[0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1

LED colors: only {0, 4, 5, 6} allowed.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 5:**

The champion: rwm=30, p=0.95 green, p=0.002 random explore→stop, p=0.0005 stop→explore back.

Trends:
1. **rwm is trending lower**: 30 beats 36, 38, 40, 43, 50. Lower turning rate = straighter movement = better coverage of arena to find green wall
2. **p=0.0002 back-transition** (rank 2, rwm=40) is very close to champion - even rarer restart is beneficial
3. **p=0.0003** (rank 4) is also good
4. **p=0.002 random trigger** remains solid
5. The 3-state variant (rank 7) is competitive despite complexity

**Exploitation focus:**
- Try rwm lower: 20, 25, 28
- Try back-transition p: 0.0001, 0.00015, 0.0002 with rwm=30
- Try p=0.002 random trigger with rwm=30 and very low back-transition
- Try p=0.0025 random trigger with rwm=30

**Exploration:**
- Try rwm=30 with p=0.0002 back (combining best rwm with best back-transition)
- 3-state: both explore states with rwm=30 and rwm=20, stop on green

**3-state machine note:** From round 5, the 3-state (rank 7) was decent. In that machine state 1 (stop) transitions to state 2 (D=1 from state 1 = state 2), state 2 transitions back to state 1 (D=1 from state 2 = state 1). This creates explore→stop→explore2→stop cycle which is fine.

Let me verify 3-state destination indexing again carefully:
- State 0: others=[1,2]. D=0→state1(stop), D=1→state2(explore2)
- State 1: others=[0,2]. D=0→state0(explore1), D=1→state2(explore2)
- State 2: others=[0,1]. D=0→state0(explore1), D=1→state1(stop)

So for the 3-state with state0=explore(30), state1=stop, state2=explore(20):
- State 0: see green→state1(D=0), random p=0.002→state1(D=0)
- State 1: random p=0.0002→state2(D=1) [go to explore2 instead of explore1]
- State 2: see green→state1(D=1), random p=0.002→state1(D=1)

LED colors allowed: {0, 4, 5, 6} only.

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 6:**

The champion: rwm=30, p=0.95 green, p=0.002 random explore→stop, p=0.0001 stop→explore back.

Clear trends:
1. **p=0.0001 back-transition** is best (even rarer restart = better)
2. **rwm=30** is still optimal, but rwm=20 (rank 3) is very close
3. **p=0.002 random trigger** remains optimal
4. The 3-state (rank 4) with rwm=30→stop→rwm=20 is excellent
5. p=0.0015 and p=0.0025 random triggers are worse than p=0.002

**Key insight:** The back-transition probability matters a lot. p=0.0001 beats p=0.0002 beats p=0.0003 beats p=0.0005. This suggests even lower (p=0.00005 or p=0.00001) might help further. But there's a floor: if p is too low, robots that stop early (before green appears) due to the random trigger can't recover.

Actually wait - the random explore→stop trigger at p=0.002 means robots stop randomly even before seeing green. The back-transition p=0.0001 means once stopped, they rarely restart. This seems like it would hurt phase 1. But it's working well - perhaps because:
- Most robots don't stop randomly before green appears (p=0.002 over ~500 steps = ~63% chance of stopping at least once, but timing varies)
- After green appears, ALL robots that see it stop immediately (p=0.95)
- The very rare back-transition means stopped robots stay stopped

**Exploitation:**
- Try back-transition p=0.00005, p=0.00001 with rwm=30
- Try rwm=20 with p=0.0001 back (combining ranks 1 and 3)
- Try rwm=25 with p=0.0001 back
- Fine-tune random trigger: p=0.002 seems locked in

**Exploration:**
- 3-state with p=0.0001 back (the rank 4 3-state used p=0.0002)
- Try p=0.003 or p=0.0015 random trigger with p=0.0001 back
- Try rwm=15 (even lower turning)

For 3-state machines (state0=explore_A, state1=stop, state2=explore_B):
- From state 0: others=[1,2]. D=0→state1(stop), D=1→state2
- From state 1: others=[0,2]. D=0→state0, D=1→state2(explore_B)
- From state 2: others=[0,1]. D=0→state0, D=1→state1(stop)

LED colors: {0, 4, 5, 6} only.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 7:**

The champion: rwm=15, p=0.95 green, p=0.002 random explore→stop, p=0.0001 stop→explore back.

Clear trends:
1. **rwm is still trending lower**: 15 beats 20 beats 25 beats 28 beats 30 beats 32. Lower turning = straighter paths = better arena coverage to find the green wall
2. **p=0.0001 back-transition** is consistently best
3. **p=0.002 random trigger** remains optimal
4. **p=0.95 green detection** unchanged
5. The 3-state machines (ranks 5, 7, 8) are competitive but not beating the champion

**Key question:** Will rwm=10 or rwm=5 be even better? Lower rwm means straighter paths, meaning robots traverse the arena more systematically and are more likely to encounter the green wall.

**Important note on the errors:** The errors about cle=2, missing n2, missing n2x0 destination keep appearing. These are from machines I did NOT submit (they may be injected by the optimizer). I must ensure all my machines are syntactically perfect.

**Exploitation focus:**
- Try rwm=10, rwm=8, rwm=5, rwm=12 (continuing downward trend)
- Try p=0.00005 back-transition (even rarer)
- Try rwm=15 with p=0.0015 and p=0.0025 random trigger (slightly different from 0.002)
- Try rwm=20 with p=0.0001 back (rank 2 was close)

**Exploration:**
- 3-state with rwm=15 as primary explore state (combining best rwm with 3-state structure)
- Try rwm=1 (almost no turning - very straight paths)

For 3-state (state0=explore_A, state1=stop, state2=explore_B):
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 8:**

The champion: rwm=1, p=0.95 green, p=0.002 random explore→stop, p=0.0001 stop→explore back. Score: 5324!

Massive jump from rwm=15 (score ~7268) to rwm=1 (score ~5324). The trend is clear: **minimum turning rate = best performance**. With rwm=1, robots move almost perfectly straight, sweeping the arena systematically and encountering the green wall with high probability quickly.

The ranking confirms: rwm=1 >> rwm=5 >> rwm=8 >> rwm=10 >> rwm=12 >> rwm=15.

**Why does rwm=1 work so well?** With straight-line movement, robots traverse the arena quickly, hit a wall, and bounce. This systematic sweeping means all robots are likely to see the green wall soon after it lights up, allowing rapid transition to stop state.

**Key parameters that are working:**
- rwm=1 (minimum turning)
- p=0.95 green detection
- p=0.002 random explore→stop trigger
- p=0.0001 back-transition (very rare restart)

**Exploitation focus:**
- rwm=1 is the minimum allowed (range [1..100]), so can't go lower
- Fine-tune p values around the champion:
  - Random trigger: try 0.001, 0.0015, 0.0025, 0.003
  - Back-transition: try 0.00005, 0.00015, 0.0002
  - Green detection: try 0.90, 0.99, 1.0
- Try rwm=2, rwm=3 (very low but not minimum)

**Exploration:**
- 3-state with rwm=1 as primary explore state
- Try having NO random trigger (only green detection) with very low back-transition
- Try p=0.002 random trigger but with p=0.00005 back

For 3-state machines:
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

# ROUND 10/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 9:**

The champion is the 3-state machine: state0=explore(rwm=1) → stop on green/random; stop → state2=explore(rwm=5) rarely; state2 → stop on green/random.

This is the final round, so I need to maximize exploitation of what we know while keeping some exploration.

**Critical findings:**
1. **3-state with rwm=1 primary and rwm=5 secondary** (rank 1, score 5298) beats pure 2-state rwm=1 (rank 4, score 5329) - barely, but consistently
2. **p=0.90 green detection** (rank 2) is surprisingly competitive with p=0.95 - suggests detection probability matters less than we thought
3. **p=0.002 random trigger** is confirmed optimal (p=0.001 rank 8, p=0.0015 rank 7, p=0.003 rank 10 all worse)
4. **p=0.0001 back-transition** is confirmed best
5. No random trigger at all (rank 11, score 7560) is catastrophically bad - the random trigger is essential for robots that never see the green wall
6. rwm=1 > rwm=2 = rwm=3 (barely distinguishable)

**Champion structure analysis:**
- State 0 (rwm=1): see green→stop(D=0); random p=0.002→stop(D=0)
- State 1 (stop): random p=0.0001→state2(D=1) [goes to rwm=5 explore]
- State 2 (rwm=5): see green→stop(D=1); random p=0.002→stop(D=1)

The key insight: after rare restart, robots switch to rwm=5 (slightly more turning) which might help them find the green wall if they were stuck in a corner.

**For this final round - exploitation:**
- Try 3-state with rwm=1/rwm=2 (instead of rwm=1/rwm=5)
- Try 3-state with rwm=1/rwm=3
- Try 3-state with rwm=1/rwm=1 (same rwm both states - effectively 2-state but with alternation)
- Vary p=0.0001 back to 0.00005 in 3-state
- Try p=0.90 in 3-state (rank 2 showed p=0.90 works well)
- Try 4-state: explore1→stop→explore2→stop2 (two stop states?)

**4-state exploration:**
State0=explore(rwm=1), State1=stop, State2=explore(rwm=5), State3=stop2
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2, D=2→state3
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2, D=2→state3
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state3
- From state 3: D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state2

Actually a simpler 4-state: explore1→stop on green; stop→explore2 rarely; explore2→stop on green; stop→explore1 rarely. But this is equivalent to the 3-state with two stop states. Not clearly better.

Let me focus on fine-tuning the 3-state champion and the best 2-state variants.

**3-state champion variations:**
1. Exact copy (champion)
2. rwm2=2 instead of 5
3. rwm2=3 instead of 5
4. rwm2=8 instead of 5
5. p=0.90 green in both states
6. p=0.00005 back-transition
7. p=0.002 vs 0.0015 random trigger in state2

**2-state variations:**
8. rwm=1, p=0.90, p=0.002, p=0.0001 (rank 2 from round 9)
9. rwm=1, p=0.95, p=0.002, p=0.0001 (rank 4, near champion)
10. rwm=1, p=0.99, p=0.002, p=0.0001 (rank 3)

**One more exploration:**
11. 3-state with p=0.002 random in state0, p=0.0015 in state2
12. 3-state: state0=explore(1), state1=stop, state2=explore(1) - same rwm, effectively testing if the 3-state structure itself helps

Let me verify the 3-state champion structure carefully:
- State 0: n0=2. Transition 0: D=0(→state1), C=7(green), l=2, p=0.95. Transition 1: D=0(→state1), C=5(random), p=0.002. ✓
- State 1: n1=1. Transition 0: D=1(→state2), C=5(random), p=0.0001. ✓  
- State 2: n2=2. Transition 0: D=1(→state1), C=7(green), l=2, p=0.95. Transition 1: D=1(→state1), C=5(random), p=0.002. ✓

From state 0 (3-state): others=[1,2]. D=0→state1, D=1→state2.
From state 1 (3-state): others=[0,2]. D=0→state0, D=1→state2.
From state 2 (3-state): others=[0,1]. D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

So in champion: state1 transition D=1→state2 ✓; state2 transitions D=1→state1 ✓. Confirmed correct.

LED colors: {0, 4, 5, 6} only!

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
